i-0a2e44a73839b312-2	Mrs. Old President! Today's debate deals with four motions for resolutions on citizenship, migration and refugee policy, family immigration and labour immigration, and concerns a large number of committee sessions and individual movements from the general period of exercise. I request the approval of Section 5 of the SfU17. Otherwise, I support the committee's proposal and reject all reservations. Mrs. Old President! At the outset, I note that the rescheduling of policies in these areas decided by the Government and the Parliament in broad agreement is also reflected in these committee reports. The policy pursued by the government has the widest possible majority and is the only one possible in view of the proposals made by all parties in Parliament. The proposals are based on the premise that there must be order and order in refugee policy. It is a policy that is well balanced according to the conditions that apply in the world around us. It is a policy based on regulated immigration that is sustainable. We cannot have a policy which differs widely from that of other countries in Europe. That is also why it is important that we push Europe for a common migration policy in which all countries take joint responsibility. Migration in the world is increasing, and Sweden is part of that development. The long-term sustainability and legal certainty of migration policies that safeguard the right to asylum is a prerequisite for managing these developments in the long term. Asylum law is a right enshrined in international conventions. The number of asylum seekers who applied to Sweden in 2015 was record high, and the challenges for the Swedish asylum system were therefore great. The situation of reception is currently under control, although there are still challenges. In order to deal with the acute refugee situation and to uphold the right of asylum, a restriction was needed in the reception of refugees in Sweden. The Swedish legal framework was therefore temporarily adapted to the minimum level of EU law and international conventions by means of temporary measures, such as temporary residence permits and temporary identity and border controls. The measures taken together with political changes in other countries and within the EU resulted in significantly fewer people applying for asylum in Sweden in 2016. According to the committee, the temporary measures taken in a very strained situation were necessary and sufficient. I would like to point out that the restrictions on the possibility of obtaining a residence permit in Sweden are temporary and that the government already intends to assess the need for the law to apply during the last year of its validity after two years. Although the government's policy has led to our being on the right track in migration policy, there are challenges. Some of these challenges have existed for a long time, and I can only regret that previous governments did not start the necessary change work. However, we are pleased that this work has now been started. This concerns, for example, the issue of age assessments, where we now have a new working order in place. The same applies to the housing issue, where all municipalities now have to share responsibility for the reception of new arrivals. This is a joint responsibility for the whole country. We now also have a government that is active in EU cooperation to influence the EU's common regulatory framework, so that all EU countries share responsibility for the refugees coming to the EU. A great deal of work is under way in all the relevant policy areas, either in ongoing investigations or in proposals prepared by the government. Many motion requests have therefore been rejected by that very argument. These include proposals concerning the European asylum system, the reception of asylum seekers, the enforcement of expulsion decisions and the issue of legal channels. When it comes to students' and researchers' opportunities to stay in Sweden, the rules have been extended. The conditions for foreign students to stay after completing studies to apply for a job or examine the conditions for doing business are now better – this is to increase Sweden's attractiveness. The committee also notes that a new directive has been adopted within the EU with rules on entry and residence for students and researchers from non-EU countries, among others. The Directive is now to be implemented in the Member States. In the Committee's view, this work should be postponed. With regard to the EU Blue Card, it is worth mentioning that a new Blue Card Directive is under way within the EU to repeal and replace the current one. The committee believes that this work should be postponed. According to the committee, there is no reason for Parliament to make an announcement on the amendment of foreigner's legislation. The government's special investigators presented in December 2016, in the report "Strengthening the position of labour migrants in the labour market", a number of proposals for measures to strengthen the position of labour migrants in the labour market. The report of the investigation also proposes that the Migration Agency should be given the possibility of direct access to certain tasks of other authorities and that the Government should be allowed to provide further regulations on the implementation of the Migration Agency and the reporting of the control of work permits. The Finnish Immigration Service is proposed to gain direct access to certain specified data from the Swedish Tax Agency, the Swedish Enforcement Authority, the Swedish Social Insurance Agency and the Swedish Pension Agency if the data are needed for handling applications for certain residence permits and work permits or for checking such work permits. The investigator points out that direct access poses specific risks of an improper breach of privacy. The question of whether an authority should have direct access to information held by another authority should therefore be assessed by balancing the grounds for such access with the grounds for privacy that contradict. The issue of combating abuse of labour immigration rules must be taken very seriously. At the same time, it can be noted that the issue has been dealt with in a recent investigation and that preparation is ongoing within the Government Office. However, the issue of direct access, as the investigator points out, is complex, and the risk of privacy restrictions must be taken into account. Deteriorated conditions and conditions for labour immigrants, such as long processing times at the Migration Agency and increased risk of having the residence permit withdrawn due to minor mistakes committed by the employer, reduces Sweden's attractiveness and risks hindering the necessary migration of skills. We assume that the government will come back with proposals as soon as possible. An announcement on this issue cannot and should not, in our opinion, speed up the thorough preparation of the issue that we consider necessary. The forms, which may be considered to be mainly satisfied, should therefore be suppressed. Mrs. Old President! With this, I call for approval of the Social Democrats' and the Green Party's joint reservation 5 in the SfU17 and also for approval of the committee's proposal.
i-0a2e44a73839b312-23	Mrs. Old President! We are going to deal with a number of reports today. I will come back to the reservations I would like to express. Let me start by saying, however, that we are being seen today in this House in the light of the fact that 300 000 people have applied for asylum in Sweden in three years. That's a figure we've never been near before. In fact, it is the highest inflow per capita ever measured in an OECD country. This is partly due to a world in crisis. Many people are on the run, for good reason. But it is also because Sweden has been allowed a long time to have regulations, laws and regulations, which differed drastically on several points from what has been the practice in our surrounding world. What has applied in most other countries – such as temporary residence permits, dependencies for family migration that have worked in practice and medical age assessments for unaccompanied persons – were for a long time distant in Sweden. It's always easy to be hindsight. But I believe that the one-track discussion on migration, cross-border mobility and target conflicts that migration policy entails has been bad for Sweden. The consequences will frame Swedish policies for many years to come, perhaps not primarily in terms of migration, but rather in terms of integration. The big question is how to avoid repeating the failed integration policies of recent decades and instead create the conditions for more people to be able to get a job, get a place to live and build a future in our country. But there is also a need for a sustainable migration policy, in which we can actually cope with the integration of all the people who have come to Sweden. Mrs. Old President! The basis of migration policy is that those who have asylum reasons should be given refuge while those who do not have it should return home. There must simply be a difference between yes and no. Just as we will have more discussions on integration in this House, this is a debate which, unfortunately, we are still only at the beginning of. Yesterday, the Finnish Immigration Service also wrote the forecast for the number of people expected to deviate from the coming years. An already troubling situation became even more troubling. The Finnish Immigration Service estimates that 51 000 new cases will be handed over from the Finnish Immigration Service to the police in the coming years. We already have around 20 000 expulsion cases today. In the last three years, 11,000 cases have been time-barred. It is, on a low scale, about 70 000 to 80 000 people. It corresponds to the size of a medium-sized, or perhaps even a little larger, Swedish city that within a few years risks being in the deepest of alienation. This is deeply problematic. It not only undermines confidence in migration policy; it also undermines the difference between yes and no. It will bring about a social vulnerability that we have never seen in this country before. Who takes responsibility for 70 000 – 80 000 people living in that shadow society? Who will take responsibility for the children who will be born into this? It's easy to be hindsight. But it is a problem that there has been an attitude in Swedish politics where it has been seen as inhumane to take hold of the emerging shadow society. It has not been dared to say that regulated immigration is based on the return of those who have no reason for asylum, otherwise there is no sustainable policy. Migration policy is difficult because it is about people. These are people who often find themselves in different forms of vulnerability. Many people have good reasons to go to Sweden, with or without asylum reasons. You want a better future. But to ignore the fundamentals of migration policy, the difference between yes and no, is not humane or long-term. Mrs. Old President! All international experience shows that to prevent such a shadow society from developing, one must do so even before the problems have grown big. When the problems are there, it's hard to handle. I question whether the government has the power and the proposals to reverse developments. We can see that the detention centres are not enough, that the immigration controls have fallen like a stone and that the return is not working. Last year, the police carried out only 3,700 errands. There were 4,000 cases that were time-barred. We are therefore prescripting more cases in this country than can be carried out by force. We have a well-being that many only dream of. This prosperity has not been created by political decisions, it has not been created by central committees and it has not been created by the state or by authorities. The prosperity we enjoy has been created by one thing: people have rolled up their sleeves and worked hard for hundreds of years. They have plowed their land, they have worked in the mine or in the factory and they have cared for their home. This has continued into our time. Of course, these mines are still there, but at the same time they have been complemented by a large number of new jobs in sectors which, perhaps only ten years ago, we did not even know would exist. For several hundred years, Sweden has been the subject of labour migration. Sometimes people have left Sweden to go to America. There were 1.2 million people who went there in the mid 1800s. More recently, however, people have come to Sweden instead. Starting after the end of the Second World War, Finns, Yugoslavians and Greeks have come to Sweden and contributed their work and made our country an even better country to live in. The then alliance government carried out an important reform to ensure that Sweden has a modern regulatory framework in place. That reform was important. Today we can stop and note that Sweden has an attractiveness that can match many other significantly larger countries when it comes to highly skilled workers. In fact, it is very fascinating and something for which there is reason to be proud. But time has also caught up with us. All regulations must be maintained, and today it does not work as well as it should. One of the reservations I intend to endorse concerns freedom of movement within the European Union. i-0a2e44a73839b312-136	Mrs. Old President! Today we are debating several of the Social Security Committee's reports on migration and asylum policy. We also need to push for a system in Europe where all Member States take joint responsibility for receiving asylum seekers. The Green Party and the Government are working hard to streamline reception, improve establishment and shorten the way to jobs and studies for new arrivals. Early intervention is a key element of this work. Many efforts are already being made to ensure that those who have come to Sweden can now learn Swedish, educate themselves or get their previous education validated to enter the labour market. We in the Green Party stand by our belief in an open Sweden that welcomes people here. That is why it is our duty to build a society that can cope with what the world looks like. Then people also come to work earlier or start businesses. Historically, Sweden has managed to grow well. Two decades after the Balkan war, we see that those who applied for sanctuary in Sweden and made it their new home country have established themselves to the extent that there are more of them who are studying further at college than native Swedes. Sweden grew and created opportunities from a challenge. The development of these issues in recent years has been dramatic. The war in Syria continues. Chemical weapons kill civilians and children. The security situation in Afghanistan is deteriorating. People continue to flee from the dictatorship of Eritrea and from many other wars and conflicts around the world. At the same time, it is becoming increasingly difficult for people on the run to reach Europe. Not only has Sweden implemented a more restrictive policy, but it applies to many countries in Europe. Border controls, closed borders with Syria and a greater desire to close people on the run are the hallmarks of European politics. Therefore, it is extremely important that Sweden works to safeguard the right to asylum, to safeguard a legally secure and effective asylum process and good reception conditions in the negotiations on migration that are now under way at EU level. The Green Party is working to make the temporarily more restrictive policy that we now have in Sweden as short a parenthesis as possible in our country's history. The law came into being in a difficult political situation for our country. The EU abdicated from its responsibility for people on the run. The opposition pushed for a policy that went far beyond what international conventions allow. The reception capacity in Sweden was very strained. The law will automatically expire in 2019, and after two years a control post will decide whether or not there is a need for legislation in the last year. We think it is important that, both in the negotiations that are going on at EU level and in the discussion that is taking place in Sweden on migration policy, we safeguard fundamental rights for people on the run. Those who today do not have the right to be reunited with their loved ones should receive it in the future. More people in need of protection should be covered by the security that it means having a permanent residence permit and knowing that you can build your future here. Currently, relatively few people come to Sweden to apply for asylum. This is not because fewer people have a need for protection. Historically many people are on the run in the world. It also depends not primarily on what legislation we have in Sweden, but more on other external factors. Border controls exist across Europe and the EU's cooperation with Turkey means that fewer people cross the Mediterranean from there. It is important that we protect the Nordic passport freedom. When the temporary ID and border controls were introduced, the reality was quite different from what it is doing today. At that time, at most about 10,000 people applied for asylum a week, and the situation was very strained on social services and at the Migration Board. Now there will be extremely few asylum seekers to Sweden every week. Internal border controls have brought together enormous difficulties both for asylum seekers without valid ID documents and for border commuters in the Öresund region. The biggest impact is the identification checks that involve changing trains for passengers passing Kastrup Airport. This prevents asylum seekers without ID documents from entering Sweden. It is well known that the objective of our party is to return to free movement within the EU and that the practical difficulties for asylum seekers posed by the identity checks can be eliminated as soon as possible. We believe it is crucial that, while the temporary law applies, we also increase reception capacity and improve establishment. Long processing times in the asylum process are a major problem, especially for the individual who is forced to live in uncertainty about his or her future for a long time, which in turn may have a negative impact on the health and establishment of the asylum seeker. The fact that waiting for asylum decisions is made as short as possible is the single most important thing for a good start in Sweden The fact that short processing times has been an important priority for the Swedish Green Party in the government. In 2016, the Government increased funding to the Finnish Immigration Service to deal with the large number of asylum seekers. It was about both increased housing costs and that the agency could increase its capacity to register and test more applications and hire more staff. One of the biggest reforms during this parliamentary term that has been implemented is that all Swedish municipalities should now take responsibility and help with the reception of refugees. The aim is to achieve a more balanced distribution of the reception of new arrivals between the municipalities of the country, and that municipalities that have good conditions for establishment should receive new arrivals to a greater extent than before. It is extremely important to follow up on this so that it is fully implemented and that all municipalities really participate. We have also carried out extra efforts in trauma care. The occurrence of diseases requiring care when arriving in Sweden has been greatly affected by experiences, traumas and risks to which the asylum seeker has been exposed during the flight. This, together with separation from family members, entails an increased risk of mental illness. At the Green Party's initiative, the government has therefore decided to invest in healthcare and treatment for traumatized asylum seekers and newly arrived asylum seekers with SEK 40 million between 2017 and 2020. In 2015, around 35 000 unaccompanied minors arrived in Sweden. Sweden made a huge effort when so many asylum seekers arrived for a short time. People stood up and became good men for unaccompanied children, and people engaged in sports clubs that organized activities in accommodations. Accommodation staff and teachers have done their utmost to welcome the young lonely guys into the community. Now, however, we see how the concern is spread among those who have come to Sweden as unaccompanied minors and among those who work with unaccompanied minors in different ways. Age write-ups seem to be arbitrary, the rejections come on stream because many who came in 2015 and are waiting for decisions now get their decisions. Readmission agreements with Afghanistan and migrating between schools and municipalities make the situation very difficult, and there is great concern. In recent months, several suicides have been reported among unaccompanied minors in asylum seekers. It is therefore positive that several important changes for this group have been made and are about to be implemented. Age assessments will be more legally secure and will take place earlier in the asylum process. A new legislation that will soon be debated and voted on in the Riksdag will allow unaccompanied minors instead of receiving temporary residence permits and risk being rejected as soon as they turn 18, now be able to complete high school in Sweden and then have time to apply for a job and in that case be able to stay permanently. This is something that the Green Party considers extremely important for the safety and mental health of these children. The Government has also recently decided to establish a national knowledge centre for issues related to unaccompanied minors at the National Board of Health and Welfare. The aim is to disseminate up-to-date knowledge, successful methods and effective processes to the municipalities, county councils and responsible authorities that implement actions for unaccompanied children. We are constantly working on measures that can improve the situation of severely vulnerable groups. During this term of office, we have also strengthened the reception of municipalities by increasing the school column for asylum seekers, by establishing the form of housing support and by providing a lot of resources to civil society organisations to carry out activities for asylum seekers during the period of asylum. We will continue to work to improve the situation of unaccompanied minors, not least with regard to mental illness and the stress of being constantly relocated between housing, schools and municipalities. Finding a job is the key to a good and fast establishment in society. It is therefore positive that the question of how more asylum seekers can work from day one is being prepared at the Government Office. The Swedish Tax Agency has also, on behalf of the Government, made proposals that give the Finnish Immigration Service the opportunity to request so-called coordination numbers for all asylum seekers. The changes to the rules that will enter into force on 1 July 2017 will probably significantly facilitate the work of the Employment Service with asylum seekers. It is important that more asylum seekers have the right to work and that obstacles to asylum seekers' access to work are removed. The situation of migrant workers also needs to be improved, not least so as to reduce processing times and so that workers do not lose their residence permits due to minor errors made by the employer. Mrs. Old President! We are now continuing to fight for the EU's common migration policy to be based on shared responsibility and respect for the right to asylum. We need to create safer systems that steer from dangerous journeys to legal routes with more quota refugees, more scholarship solutions, labour migration and other legal ways of travelling safely and safely. At the same time, we see political counter-reactions and more solitude. More border barriers and walls are being built up. Restrictive policies are expensive in both economic and political and humane terms, and there are plenty of deterrent examples around the world. Australia's refugee camp in Nauru has been lined with sexual abuse, violence, malnutrition, and self - injury. US border controls cost more than all other law enforcement authorities. The EU's agreement with Turkey gives an autocratic regime increased power over Europe, and we can see in what direction Turkey is going right now. A restrictive approach is costly and extremely difficult to reconcile with human rights. The Center Party stands for openness, and we continue to strive for freer mobility. But it must be done in a humane and orderly manner. There are limits to how much an individual country can do. Let me say it again, because I can imagine that I am getting lines on exactly this: there are limits to how much an individual country can do. As long as immigration costs reception, there will be a balance that each country must make. One way to alleviate the problem is, of course, to strive to reduce costs and improve establishment. When the government defends all costly benefits but denies people their right to seek protection, we in the Center Party protect the right to seek protection but not all benefits. That is our way in this. For us, it is important to safeguard transparency and humanism. We stand up for the right to seek protection, the right to family reunification, security for asylum seekers and effective integration. We see migration as something fundamentally positive. At the same time, we are not turning a blind eye to the problems that exist or the major reforms that are needed. Much of this, including integration and establishment, is dealt with in other committees. But even in our area, in the Social Security Committee, there are things to deal with. The government's policy has severely limited family immigration and means that many families will not be legally able to reunite for several years. Family immigration is one of the few legal routes to Europe. It is of great importance for humanitarian reasons. Families divided as a result of war, conflict and oppression must have an opportunity to live together. With increasing globalisation, where more and more people have loved ones in other countries, family immigration is also increasing in importance. We in the Center Party want to replace the strong restriction by a more humane reform which nevertheless goes in line with the migration agreement concluded between the Alliance and the Government in autumn 2015. Several have spoken about labour migration. This is because the situation in this area is acute at the moment. Labour migration is an asset for Sweden's skills supply, for society's economy and for the competitiveness of companies. In addition, labour migration means a safe way here for people who have the opportunity to contribute with skills, knowledge and important work. It is no loss for Sweden to make it easier for people who can make a living to stay in the country. For us, it is extremely important to act against any proposal that would make labour migration more difficult. These are still untenably long processing times, the rules are complicated and minimal deficiencies can lead to expulsion. Yet the government has only put forward policies to make it more difficult. There are still no proposals to make things easier, even though it has previously been promised, both in committee and in the media, to do something about the situation. This is totally unacceptable. Thousands of hardworking people in this country are now in uncertainty and insecurity. A lot of them will be in touch with me. I get new emails every week about this. At the same time, more and more companies continue to be alarmed about the desperation of the situation. It's just getting worse and worse. Finally, I would like to raise an issue that is becoming increasingly urgent every day. Recently, at least four unaccompanied young people from Afghanistan have killed themselves. Many more have tried to commit suicide or harm themselves in other ways. Since last spring, the Migration Agency has written up many people of age according to previous routines. Those who have been considered to be over 18 years of age under this procedure have, as a result, usually received expulsion decisions. The National Board of Forensic Medicine has developed a more secure management, and medical age assessments have now started to be carried out on a large scale. That's good. With more and better knowledge of age assessments, the trials become less arbitrary and fairer than they used to be. However, many have already received their expulsion decisions according to the old legal uncertainty method. This means that there is a significant risk that minors who are in Sweden have been wrongly assessed as adults and should therefore be expelled. We in the Center Party want those who have already received age to be offered the opportunity to go through the more legally secure medical age assessment in order to reduce the risk of minors being expelled. We also want the age you had when the application was submitted to apply to decisions and that you should be allowed to stay in your municipality until the decision has gained legal force if you are under 21 years of age. We have an enormous responsibility in this House to increase the security and confidence of this group in the future. Of course, I support all our reservations, but for the time to come I only support the Center Party's reservation 2 in SfU15 and reservation 2 in SfU16, the alliance joint reservations 1 and 4 in SfU17 and the Center Party's reservation 3 in SfU17.

i-0a2e44a73839b312-520	Madam President, I would like Migration policy, which we have in focus for this debate on the four reports SfU14, SfU15, SfU16 and SfU17, we have had the opportunity and opportunity to discuss and debate many times in recent years. It has not least been about the migration agreement, about breathing space and refugee break, about border and ID checks, about the temporary law and about the negotiations on a new harmonised migration policy within the EU. We in the Christian Democrats have, of course, kept to this, but we have also come up with a lot of our own proposals and set out our own direction for how we want Sweden's migration policy to be shaped. We have put forward proposals which, on the basis of the right of asylum, have aimed, and continue to aim, at achieving an orderly, long-term responsible and humane migration policy that offers refuge for people on the run. In my speech today, I intend to highlight some key elements of this whole which are re-emerged in the four motions for resolutions. Firstly, in order to improve the reception of asylum seekers and to make the asylum process more efficient, we have proposed the establishment of asylum application areas near our major border crossing points and the reference to all those who intend to apply for asylum in Sweden. In these areas, the registration of asylum applications shall be carried out and accommodation for asylum seekers shall also be provided during the first phase of the asylum investigation. The idea is that persons who obviously do not help to prove their identity and who come from so-called safe countries, or who, in accordance with the rules of the Dublin Regulation, should have their asylum application examined in another country, should be given a rapid expulsion decision. The same applies to persons who are deemed to be a safety risk. The establishment of asylum application areas would contribute to a comprehensive reception and decision-making process, thereby preventing people from digressing and ending up in a vulnerable situation. Madam President, I would like Secondly, the Christian Democrats have also proposed the introduction of an asylum programme of six months. The idea behind this is to reduce inaction during the period of asylum, and to speed up both the establishment of the labour market and integration in Sweden by early communication of the norms and values that characterise our society. It is important to make it clear that people who come to Sweden with the intention of staying will learn the Swedish language and our culture. Of course, the starting point is to support oneself and your family. We therefore believe that participation in formal education and social orientation should be offered and required at the beginning of the asylum period. In order for more asylum seekers to work during the period of asylum, we also want a working requirement for asylum seekers to be available in the labour market. At the same time, we have proposals both to make it easier for asylum seekers to work and to strengthen asylum seekers' incentives to work. Child care shall be offered to ensure that the asylum programme also works for parents of young children, while at the same time allowing those who do not participate in the asylum programme to have their daily allowance reduced. Madam President, I would like Thirdly, the Christian Democrats want to see a permanent law with the meaning that all groups of persons in need of protection should initially be granted a limited three-year residence permit. If the reasons for protection persist after these three years, or if the person concerned has acquired a job or started his or her own business on which he or she can make a living, a permanent residence permit shall be granted. But the Christian Democrats have also said that the right to family reunification should also apply to those who need protection who have a temporary residence permit, because it is better that the people who have come to Sweden focus on establishing themselves in the labour market and in society as soon as possible than that they focus on worrying about their relatives. We are fully aware that this has financial implications, so in order to finance the deviation from government policy on the possibility of family reunification, we have set aside SEK 1.3 billion in our shadow budget for this year. At the same time, we believe that the right to family immigration should apply only to the relatives of whom the asylum seeker has informed at the time of arrival, both to facilitate the identification of asylum seekers and to gain better control of their reception. Madam President, I would like Fourthly, it is important to stress that those who have come to Sweden to seek asylum but who, after an individual and legal review, are not deemed to have sufficient grounds for protection to obtain a residence permit as quickly as possible must return to their home country. Unfortunately, we know that both the return that takes place on its own and the forced expulsions and expulsions that take place are working too badly today. In order to overcome this, it is important that Sweden enters into more readmission agreements with the main countries of origin to receive their citizens. However, more resources are also needed for the police to be able to execute expulsions and expulsions, and also to a greater extent to carry out internal checks on aliens. More detention sites are also needed. We have also set aside money for this in our budget for this year. Fifthly, the Christian Democrats are working to ensure that there are more legal ways into Sweden to live and work here. Together with efforts to strengthen Sweden's competitiveness by facilitating the recruitment of employees from all over the world as needed, this is the main reason why we want to facilitate labour migration. It should be easy to work and to do the right thing in Sweden. This requires that rigid rules or unnecessary bureaucracy do not put obstacles in the way. As all my alliance colleagues have said here in the speaker's chair earlier today, there is every reason to look at the overly rigid rules that have led to people's unreasonable rejection of their applications for work permits. We believe that an overall assessment should be made in these cases and that employers who recognise a mistake made and want to correct it afterwards should be able to do so. It is a clear demand from the KD and the Alliance that the government should return as soon as possible with proposals for rules that make it easier for labour immigrants to do the right thing and to stay in Sweden. Madam President, I would like Sixthly, and finally, I would like to say a few words about Swedish citizenship. Swedish citizenship does not mean anything. This means many rights but also obligations. These include the right to vote in parliamentary elections, the right to seek certain types of services in the police, the court and the military, the unconditional right to reside in the country and the possibility of consular assistance abroad. With citizenship, the citizen also becomes part of something in common, where the sense of participation, belongingness and identity is strengthened. At the same time, it is important to emphasise that citizenship also entails obligations. It is about taking responsibility for society by contributing to and promoting the common good. It is about complying with the laws and taking responsibility for the governance of society by participating in the democratic elections. The Christian Democrats believe that the importance of citizenship should be enhanced. Among other things, we want to do this by introducing requirements for applicants for citizenship to undergo education in social orientation and to gain knowledge of Swedish society. We also think that a citizenship book should be submitted to the new Swedish citizens in connection with the citizenship ceremonies organised in the municipalities. The new citizens would then be better and more actively able both to exercise the rights and to fulfil the obligations of citizenship. Having said that, Madam President, I would like to make it clear that the Christian Democrats naturally support all our reservations in the four reports we are dealing with today. However, for time to come, we in this House are only calling for reservation 3 in SfU14, for reservation 5 in SfU15 and for the alliance reservations 1 and 4 in SfU17.

Many entrepreneurs are adversely affected. They testify to problems in getting rid of day funds, how difficult it is to get change, how far they are forced to go in order to be able to do business with bank services regarding cash and how they feel about their business threatened by the lack of cash. Many of these businesses are located in rural areas, where access to broadband does not always exist. They also have many older customers who want to use cash. It should, as the Riksbank proposes, be a flexible legislation aimed at the banks that is adapted to different banks' target groups. We see that it works in Norway, and then it should work just as well here. i-JwicAYWQPStg535yAtQjd2	Madam President, I would like This report is primarily about implementing the EU directive called the Payment Account Directive. The bill proposes that the Payment Services Act be amended and, in part, some changes to the Marketing Act. The bill contains three parts. Firstly, rules on fee information aim to facilitate consumer transparency and comparability of fees charged for payment accounts. Secondly, the proposal addresses the issue of facilitating the exchange of payment accounts between different payment service providers. The third area dealt with by the bill is the consumer's access to payment accounts with basic features. Credit institutions providing a payment account with basic features shall offer it to all consumers. In addition to these rules, there are also rules on supervision and sanctions. Madam President, I would like Everyone shall be able to obtain a payment account with basic features upon application, and it shall be provided promptly. A payment account is defined as an account used to carry out payment transactions. An individual credit institution cannot refuse access to the payment services provided by the credit institution to an individual consumer. Furthermore, it may not be conditional that the acquisition of a payment account requires the purchase of other services held by the credit institution. The committee believes that there is no need to introduce rules requiring all credit institutions to offer all basic functions. The committee believes that it is enough that there are enough institutions to provide the various services. Madam President, I would like The application for a payment account shall be dealt with promptly by credit institutions. The committee believes that it should take ten business days for the Institute to announce decisions. If the decision is rejected, the consumer shall be informed immediately and shall be informed of where he or she may bring a complaint and where he or she may have the decision brought before the courts. The application may be rejected if the payment account is in breach of the Anti-Money Laundering and Terrorist Financing Act. It can also be rejected if there are special reasons. Specific reasons may include money laundering and unfair behaviour of the consumer towards any credit institution. The account can only be terminated if the account is used for illegal purposes, if the account is not used for 24 months and if the consumer has provided incorrect information in the application. Credit institutions shall notify the consumer in writing with a notice period of at least two months. Furthermore, reference shall be made to where the consumer can seek redress and lodge a complaint. The committee does not believe that a move outside the EU or that the consumer has an account in another credit institution is a reason for dismissal. Madam President, I would like The committee shares the government's view that credit institutions and banks should have clear procedures for consumers' switching of payment service providers across the EU and the EEA. Credit institutions shall assist in the transfer of funds and the closure of accounts. Credit institutions may charge reasonable fees for the costs associated with the change of payment service provider. However, these shall correspond to the costs incurred by credit institutions for the transfers. The Swedish Bankers' Association has established rules for its members that are simpler than what is stated in the directive. The Bankers' Association's recommendation is a faster and easier way to handle exchanges. The committee therefore believes that the possibility that the directive allows to apply existing rules should be applied in Sweden. The committee believes, however, that it should be stipulated that credit institutions should have procedures for exchanging payment accounts. In the event of damage to property in the event of a change and caused by the credit institution, the institution shall compensate the consumer. Madam President, I would like Credit institutions have the possibility to charge a reasonable fee for the payment account. The consumer shall be provided with price information on the fees and interest resulting from payment services. The directive states that certain payment accounts with limited access to payment services should be offered free of charge, but the committee believes that this is not necessary on the Swedish market. In addition, the consumer shall have access to free websites to compare prices in different credit institutions. Madam President, I would like Finally, the proposal contains a number of rules on dispute resolution, supervision and sanctions. The committee notes that disputes are to be dealt with by existing ADR bodies, i.e. the General Complaints Board. According to the Payment Account Directive, there must be a supervisory authority, and the committee recommends that Finansinspektionen become this authority. Madam President, I would like In connection with the payment services inquiry, the Riksbank replied that it was assessed that there was at that time an imbalance in the supply of cash services and that this was largely due to the rapid reduction in the banks' cash management. The Riksbank recommends that credit institutions be given a responsibility for having access to cash in society. Furthermore, the Riksbank considers that the Payment Account Directive should also cover associations and companies. The committee considers that obtaining payment services is not such a problem for companies and associations as to give rise to legislative action. Madam President, I would like There has been a motion in this report which explicitly addresses the need for cash issues. On the one hand, the exerciser wants society to be obliged to provide and receive cash and, on the other hand, the government to set up an investigation to review whether large banks can be required to set up ATMs in rural areas and that associations and businesses should be subject to the right to open payment accounts with basic payment services. In addition to this, there is a special statement from the four bourgeois parties about the problems in society – that it is problematic to withdraw cash but also that individuals, companies and associations can also not hand in their day funds and money. Madam President, I would like I agree with much of the particular opinion of the bourgeois parties. There is a problem with the banks having decommissioned their cash operations at a very rapid rate. It is therefore important that a fundamental analysis be carried out of the most appropriate measures to enable cash to exist. This requires an investigation, and both the Riksbank and Finansinspektionen have presented a number of ideas on how this can be resolved. The committee notes that the recent investigation into the Riksbank has the task of investigating this. Tightly it can be interpreted as referring only to the Riksbank's role in cash handling. However, statements by members of the investigation suggest that the aim is to find a satisfactory solution to the problem. With this, Madam President, I support the committee's proposal and reject the motion.

i-0a2e44a73839b312-636	Mr. Old President! The report that we are going to discuss today deals with a number of issues, but I am going to focus on the issue that has perhaps been most debated here this afternoon, and that is access to cash and its handling. The problems are well known. In both rural and urban suburbs, more and more bank accounts have either disappeared completely or ended up in cash. As a result, people who use cash in their daily lives find it more difficult to do so. It is more difficult to withdraw as well as deposit cash. An equally important problem is that companies and associations that receive or handle a lot of cash in their business find it difficult to get rid of them. There are examples of tourist companies in northern Sweden who are forced to drive hundreds of thousands of miles of cash in order to deposit these cash funds at all in a bank or similar. It is a problem that needs to be resolved. In principle, it has two solutions. Firstly, we must continue with the roll-out of digital infrastructure, because no one should think that it is something temporary that we use less and less cash and that fewer and fewer people want to deal with it. The development with cards and Swish and the like will continue, and it is basically very good if and when everything works. However, this is based on the availability of broadband and mobile coverage. It does not exist in all parts of the country, and it is a problem that needs to be resolved. Another problem that needs to be resolved is, of course, the actual handling of cash. We will, albeit to a small extent, have to use cash and continue to want to use cash. We will receive visitors from all over the world for whom it is natural to use cash. There will therefore be a need for a well-functioning cash management system for the foreseeable future. Then the only question is how to get it in place. For me, the question is relatively simple. We already have today a system of safe transport between various forms of depots and banks and trade, both on and off. Of course, this is what we need to start from. There is today a shared responsibility between a fairly broad variety of authorities such as the Swedish Post and Telecom Agency, county boards and the Riksbank. It is a responsibility shared with, for example, banks, trade and other actors. It is natural that it is in this circle, possibly in a more pure circle, that the problem is also solved. Then comes the questions of who sets the guidelines, who pays and who in the individual cases has that last main responsibility. There is a reason to simply put your foot down and agree on what should apply. This issue has been investigated on several occasions. What is now needed is that we agree and establish a system that will apply in the future and that works better than today and erases the white spots found on the Swedish cash handling map at present. The discussion has largely focused on what kind of investigation is the best way to solve the problem. I think most people are less interested in it. We forced a parliamentary inquiry into surplus targets and the like and took the opportunity to resolve a number of issues that, for example, had bearing on the budgetary process. In the near future, the investigation that will review the Riksbank Act regarding everything from the Board of Directors and inflation targets to cash management will have its first meeting. Since I am part of the investigation, I will propose at that meeting that we write an interim report as a matter of urgency, that we send the signal from all parties in Parliament – at best – to the primary government that now we have to solve the problem. I think it is the fastest and most effective way to get there, and that is why we choose to act. This issue is not served by placarding in committee initiatives. What counts seriously is what solves the problem most quickly and best, and that is exactly what the Center Party and the Alliance intend to do.

i-0a2e44a73839b312-677	Mr. Old President! I support Amendment No 1, and otherwise I support the report on behalf of the Green Party. Today we can say that we have a patent mouse test ahead of us, which is about whether we are serious about phasing out fossil fuels from the transport sector – yes or no. This concept, which we have talked about many times in this House, was launched in an annex to a budget proposal from the Alliance Government. In the past, there was an overall climate target for the whole transport sector, but under the alliance government the target was changed: a fossil fuel independent vehicle fleet. It was a little unclear what this meant, thought many, including the then government, so an investigation was set up that resulted in a report: Fossil Freedom on the Move, SOU 2013:84. This report we debated last week, and I believe that no one said anything but that it is clear that we do not want a future transport system that is vulnerable, that has a negative impact on the climate or that is causing environmental damage to people, which the burning of oil actually gives rise to in the form of harmful air pollution. However, it is one thing to support an overall objective and quite another to support the measures that lead to it. Although there is much more to be done, I would like to say with determination that the Cooperation Government has launched several different proposals which, if implemented, reduce dependence on fossil fuels in the transport sector. We also have before us a new national plan for 2018 – 29, where the government has good opportunities to make it easier for both companies and individuals to move with less climate impact by concentrating on developing new main roads and railways. But what we are going to debate today are road and vehicle issues, and how does this relate to each other? Well, it is connected in that it is about the distribution of transport between road, rail and maritime. Energy consumption differs drastically, and the amount of fossil fuels for transport of the same number of tonnes is very different depending on whether one uses road, rail or shipping. In fact, shipping is best in that friction is very small when a ship meets water, if the ship does not have too high speed. Then comes the railway. Steel wheels against rails are also very energy efficient. For rubber wheels against asphalt, on the other hand, and even more for aircraft against air, the energy consumption is much greater. That is the background or main reason why the cooperation government says that it is important that the distribution between road, rail and maritime is such that dependence on fossil fuels is reduced. We may not be able to boast that this is the cooperation government's own discovery, but this has been written in reports from the Swedish Environmental Protection Agency, Trafikverket, Vägverket– well, basically all, for a very long time: To achieve climate targets and reduce dependence on oil, it is important to reduce long-distance truck transport and promote a better combination of road, rail and shipping. How do you do that? The FFF investigation, which I can understand was the flagship of the Center Party under the previous government, speaks very clearly of the 2012 Swedish National Audit Office saying that an increase in truck transport and a strong growth in freight transport according to available knowledge is likely to be difficult to reconcile with the climate targets. The Finnish Transport Agency points out that traffic development is not compatible with the climate targets if the projected increase in long-distance road transport is taken into account. This is why the study proposes various things: firstly, mileage taxes for truck transport, but also other instruments aimed at improving the competitive position of rail and maritime transport with regard to road transport. What, then, is the government's proposal? Yes, the government is interested in the climate goals but also that it works well for business, entrepreneurship and employment. Is this possible to combine? Yes, it does. It has been looked at where it might be useful for trucks to be heavier and has seen that it is primarily when transporting forest raw materials for processing. There, in principle, at the points pointed out in the Swedish Transport Administration's investigation, rail and shipping are not available alternatives. The government has therefore welcomed the introduction of a new load class in order to be able to use longer or heavier trucks in this particular context. We point out that it is a matter of caring for the roads, but also of ensuring that the increased opportunities for heavy goods vehicles do not lead to transport being taken from shipping and rail. Why do we do that? That's because the roads are actually not just for trucks. For people outside the cities, the roads may be crucial to getting to and from work or study and to socializing and meeting. The roads are also for two-wheeled vehicles with and without motor and for buses. This must be taken into account when drawing up transport rules. It seems to me very strange that in this context the Alliance has so little respect for the fact that rural people need roads for their own transport. We have every reason to take responsibility for the business community, but it is actually the people who live outside the cities who need long-term sustainable roads. I didn't know much about this when I entered the Riksdag in 1998, because in Skåne we don't have frost damage on the roads; we don't have such a climate. But in quite a large part of Sweden it is important to protect the road when it commutes between plus and minus and is partially frozen and not bit by bit. Then the roads are fragile. This is why many countries are resisting heavier transport: it damages not only the surface of the road, but also the road body itself. A few years ago I wrote a parliamentary motion on long-term sustainable roads, and then I had reason to understand these connections a little more. We should respect this very much so that we do not increase the weight of lorries except when it is really necessary. This assessment has been made by the government in the proposal put forward, and this is also what we support in reservation No 1. This is the lacquer mouse paper: Is it serious about fossil freedom on the way or not? One cannot say, on the one hand, that we want to reduce the use of fossil fuels in freight transport and, on the other hand, that one mode of transport should have greater capacity without any change for the other two. Then you don't mean it. I would also like to raise another issue. It's about bus traffic. Fortunately, there is an increased demand for bus services, and periodically buses find it difficult to accommodate enough passengers. Therefore, the Government has commissioned the Swedish Transport Agency to investigate the possibility of having trailers or longer buses. It is quite common in Germany and some other countries. It seems to me quite cost-effective that, instead of always driving on long buses, you can have a trailer bus at peak hours. This has been investigated by the Swedish Transport Agency, and I know that the matter is being prepared in the Government Office. It is a point that we allow bus services to be competitive. It is a point to take into account the speed of the different modes of transport, if we are serious about people being able to switch car commuting for public transport commuting. It would then seem inappropriate to limit the speed to 60 kilometres per hour on roads which allow considerably higher speeds for car traffic. From the point of view of road safety, it is also a point that the different vehicles have as much speed as possible. Longer buses are considerably safer than double-decks, especially in rural areas, where there can be a lot of wind. We experience it in Skåne. We like to ride with our new double tires, Skåneexpressen, but they are very wind sensitive. Perhaps some of them would be longer buses instead of higher ones.

I called them back, but no, they still haven't received a waiver. I asked the m: Do you continue to drive with 3 times 40 feet? They said: We have even had attempts where we have driven 5 times 40 back and forth. It is 5 by 12 meters, that is, 60 meters of container with a tow truck. In addition, A-tractors are built from approved inspected cars, so some crash safety hazard is not with them. They're stable vehicles. There are also other advantages to increasing speed. i-0a2e44a73839b312-809	Mr. Old President! It is therefore time for a really good proposal for a majority decision in the Swedish Parliament. The proposal in the report actually means that Parliament will support the introduction of a new weight class, BK4, which increases the maximum load for Swedish cars and lorries from 64 to 74 tonnes for heavy commercial road transport. That's really good. In addition, this report calls on the government to step up its efforts to establish a regulatory framework for longer vehicles, up to 30 metres, on the Swedish road network. This position is good for development, for the Swedish base industry, for the forest, for the mineral industry, for the Swedish engineering industry, for jobs and for the climate. It means a political ambition for the whole country to live. As the representative of the Center Party in this debate, Mr President-in-Office, I am very pleased. The purpose of the legislative proposal is to make it possible to use heavier vehicle combinations than today. This will improve the efficiency of road transport. The new load class makes it possible to increase the quantity of goods on the individual vehicle. This means that fewer vehicles will be needed to transport the same quantity of goods. This will enable emissions from road transport to be reduced while reducing the transport costs of industry and increasing competitiveness and Swedish jobs. Mr. Old President! There was a catch in the government's original bill, a catch that the parliamentary majority will now change. In the original bill, the government stated that the opening of a road network for heavier vehicles should be guided by the principle that the transfer of goods from road to rail and maritime transport should not be threatened. This may sound good, but the practical meaning of the directives given by the Government to the Finnish Transport Administration was that only 8 per cent of the Swedish public road network was divided into five distinct geographical areas. Only that much would be allocated to trucks with a maximum gross weight of 74 tonnes. The industry, which was set to use this, called it the yarn ball without transport logistics. It is possible that it is an expression of lobbying when the forest industry, the transport industry and the engineering industry call it the ball without mutual transport logistics. As a representative of the Swedish Parliament, I think it is important also to listen to the heavy industry. Mr. Old President! This should be done at the rate that road load can withstand the higher weight limits. The system must be coherent. The release must be in line with what the bearings allow. It is clear to everyone that restrictions other than the carrying capacity of the road are a mere catch-up policy, almost hokuspocus. If the road network becomes as empty as the government wanted, very few, if any, will find it meaningful to invest in modern, transport-efficient, climate-smart and larger vehicles. What, then, has led the government to put forward a fundamentally good proposal for an increased bearing class, but with such a crazy and for all outsiders incomprehensible limitation? Only 8% of the public road network would be covered. Yes, there was an ambition that this restriction should minimise the risk of transferring goods from rail and shipping to road transport. The logic of that restriction is like arguing that railway tracks should now be demolished in order to thus benefit shipping. As a center party, I believe that Sweden, the Swedish base companies and the people need all modes of transport in combination with each other. Of course, Sweden's transport policy cannot be based on the assumption that the modes of transport are competing with each other. Instead, the different modes of transport complement each other, much as Jimmy Ståhl described the example of Jula, with a combination of maritime transport, rail transport and truck transport to the final destination. That's how logistics is supposed to work. To make shipping and rail competitive by punishing road traffic is, in my opinion, a very crazy way to go. In addition, it is economically inefficient. Everyone understands that transfers between modes of transport take place in several directions and that good road connections are often a prerequisite for goods being transhipped to shipping and rail. Timber transport by truck in Smålands inland or Norrland inland provides ground for the transport of timber by rail to the coast, where most industries are located. By cutting off the possibility of transport efficiency in one mode, the whole is sabotaged. Who can even think of the absurd idea? Similar results have been found in experiments with vehicles of up to 30 metres for timber transport. I believe that those trials are made in the same constituency from which Mr Pettersson comes. We have a government that is passive in the EU context and does not seem to understand the seriousness of the proposals that are now under negotiation at EU level on climate and land use, among other things. These are proposals that may to a very large extent affect or even restrict Swedish forestry and our national self-determination over the forest. Mrs. Old President! I just stressed the importance of a balanced forestry policy. The starting point for the Moderates is the Forest Protection Act's two equal goals on the environment and production. Measures are needed to boost forest growth and measures are needed to further develop nature considerations in forestry and biodiversity. Often the debate on nature conservation is reduced to a debate on hectares – hectares of protected nature and forests. It is unfortunate, on the one hand, because the most important thing for forests and biodiversity is not how much forest is protected but that forestry is conducted in a sustainable way, and, on the other hand, because the desire to constantly exclude more forests tends to make the work of managing and managing already set aside forests and land forgotten or not prioritised. It is serious that a large part of Sweden around 4 500 nature reserves have management plans that have not been followed or that have become obsolete. That is why, Madam President-in-Office of the Council, I am in favour of Amendment No 3, which deals with this very issue. We Moderates believe that more valuable forests and nature need to be protected, but at least as important is therefore to manage already protected nature in a good way. Otherwise, not only valuable natural values fail, but also the legitimacy of politics. Mrs. Old President! Let me return to natural considerations in the very use of the forest, to what is used to be called general consideration. Forestry conservation work has taken seven million years since the current Forestry Act came into force in 1994, and this development has taken place on a voluntary basis and has been driven, among other things, by environmental certifications in forestry. Today, when felling is done, a wide range of considerations are generally taken into account and a variety of measures are taken to protect valuable nature or to create environments and structures conducive to biodiversity. In concrete terms, this includes saving valuable trees, creating dead wood, preventing road injuries, leaving edge zones next to water and much more. In addition to general considerations, which can be described as everyday considerations that are constantly taken into account in the implementation of various measures in the forest, Sweden's forest owners also make very large voluntary provisions for land. So far, Sweden's forest owners have set aside 1, 3 – 1, 4 million hectares of productive forest land, entirely voluntarily, in order to protect cores and other areas with high natural values. Mrs. Old President! Does the environmental considerations in the forest work? Is it yielding results? The answer to both questions is yes. Research from, among others, Skogforsk and SLU shows that the measures taken in forestry, such as the creation of top men and the leaving of so-called consideration areas, have an effect. Looking at important indicators to follow up on the environmental quality target Living forests, you can see that development is moving in the right direction. There is increasingly hard dead wood and more older deciduous forest. Other indicators also point in the right direction. We Moderates think it is important to continue to develop and follow up environmental considerations in forestry in order to ensure that the right measures are taken. We believe that results and efficiency also need to be at the heart of this area. Mrs. Old President! I would also like to highlight the need for the government to review the application and design of the Species Protection Regulation as well as the management of key biotopes. If in part this has already been announced to the government by a parliamentary majority, and just recently the LRF called the Forest owners together with the five major forest owners' associations in an open letter to the Prime Minister. The letter describes a growing concern about legal uncertainty and unpredictability in the exercise of authority. This must be taken seriously by the government and addressed. Finally, Madam President-in-Office, I would like to return to the enormous potential of forests and forestry. Forests are one of our most important keys to a more climate-smart and sustainable society. Let us use that key and open up for a growing bio-based economy.

For example, the so-called Species and Habitats Directive takes too far-reaching measures to protect species that are not under threat in Sweden, which is an unreasonable regime. Mrs. Old President! A generation later on can be considered to have become so valuable that they have received a protective stamp. An important lesson is that in a very short time we can create valuable natural environments. Nature's kind of working on it. A harvested forest may well contain great natural values. The creation of the key biotopes has been largely detrimental to many landowners. They often end up in an expropriation-like situation without the possibility of reasonable compensation. But the beautiful forest has not come about by chance, by nature's whim, but it is often the care and long-termness of generations. It is a threat to ownership when a forest environment where the owner himself has created the high natural values is taken from the owner precisely because of its high natural values. Ownership is being eroded by the current management of key biotopes. The industry cannot buy wood from forests classified as possible key biotopes on the basis of certification bodies' requirements. For there to be a financial settlement for the landowner, an area identified as a possible key biotope must be protected in a formal agreement. The original purpose of the Forest Board's mission to inventory key biotopes was to acquire knowledge of how forest areas should be prioritised in terms of official protection. And although the Swedish Forest Board decides not to create formal protection for a possible key biotope, it still remains in lists of key biotopes and thus in practice cannot be harvested. New positions on possible key biotopes are very rarely taken, and procedures for removal of possible key biotopes that do not lead to formal protection are lacking today. Furthermore, it has been shown that the key biotopes within the same region often contain the same biodiversity for red-listed species. This indicates, on the one hand, that many species that have been the basis for the creation of key biotopes may eventually be removed from the Red List, since they are significantly more common than we have known to date, and, on the other hand, that unnecessarily many biotopes protected have similar species compositions of rare species within the region. The Swedish Forest Board has recently made a forward-looking decision by freezing the key biotop inventory in northwestern Sweden, from Torsby Municipality in Värmland and up across the country. The difficulties encountered in applying the key biotopes concept are highlighted here by the fact that this area is characterised by large contiguous areas of old forest, where the limits of individual key biotopes are often liquid and diffuse. The decision of the Board of Forestry had to endure a great deal of unneeded criticism. Environmentally-involved researchers signed a petition, and a number of officials at the Swedish Forest Board chose to conduct political environmental activism by publicly disassociating themselves from management decisions. Most of the criticism has been based on the rather erroneous view that large areas with a protected forest are now at risk of being felled because the Swedish Forest Board during the break would not identify and delimit new key biotopes. Consciously or not, the cards are mixed away by producing the concept of key biotope as a protective form. Last Tuesday, the Swedish Forest Agency decided to establish a new package of measures for key biotopes in northwestern Sweden. In order to ensure knowledge acquisition, the Swedish Forest Board is now continuing the inventory of areas with key biotopes quality and other forests with high natural values in the area. In parallel, the work on methodological development for the goal of completing the process will be strengthened as early as 2017. It is worth noting that during this period no new areas such as key biotopes are defined. I would call this a cautious and, in our opinion, cowardly step back by the Forest Board. The task of the Forest Board has never been to delimit key biotopes, nor to use key biotopes to prevent forest felling. For such measures, the Forest Board has access to other tools. Mrs. Old President! Finally, the Swedish Democrats believe that one of the fundamental principles of society's prosperity is respect for the individual's property. The Swedish forest model with two overall and equal forest policy objectives, the environmental objective and the production target, will be preserved and further developed in consultation with the forest industry. We believe that the most successful forestry policy is achieved through voluntary and information rather than through detailed laws and regulations.
