i-0a2e44a73839b312-1141	Mrs. Old President! We are debating forestry policy today. It is an important policy area, because the forest is important to all of us. Some of us love to pick mushrooms and berries. Others like to take a run or walk with the dog in the woods. Some people like to hunt. Many gather power and recover in the forest. The forest also provides renewable materials that are needed in the sustainable construction of society. Many important social interests exist in the forest, but at the same time all Swedish forests are owned by someone. Forest owners can be anything from a private person who owns a few hectares to a large company with gigantic forest areas. And one fifth of the forest is state or municipal, so we're all owners of it. Forests provide important ecosystem services, nature's free services. Central ecosystem services include those already mentioned – health and renewable raw materials – but also air and water purification. The forest also stabilizes the climate and has a smoothing effect on temperature, precipitation and runoff. It also contains a rich biodiversity and has great beauty values. Social and private interests in the forest must be managed in a sustainable and systematic manner, respecting different views on forest values. However, the key to how forests must be managed and used is that forest ecosystems retain their ecological function, biodiversity and production capacity. It is a prerequisite for long-term sustainable, resilient and productive ecosystems. Today's use of the forest must not deteriorate tomorrow. Mrs. Old President! That society regulates the use of a resource such as the forest is necessary and justified because, as has been said, its use has a huge impact on society as a whole both now and in the future. Natural resources such as land, water and forests are mankind's common heritage from previous generations, which we borrow and will return to the future generations. The owner of a natural resource manages it for a period of time and has a responsibility for its sustainable use. Society creates rules for this, and these rules must ensure that farming is sustainable in the long term. However, current regulations and practices in the use of forests do not lead to this. This is reflected in the government's follow-up to environmental objectives and in scientific studies on the state of biodiversity in the forest landscape. A number of improvements are therefore needed in forestry, both as regards forestry methods and as regards the protection of forests with high natural values. Mrs. Old President! Forest raw materials, as many have already said, are central to a sustainable society. The Green Party wants to protect a long-term profitable and sustainable use of the forest. The regulatory systems governing use must be understandable and legally secure for landowners and possible for society to follow up and evaluate. The Forestry Act is an important instrument for achieving environmental objectives. According to the Environmental Protection Agency's in-depth evaluation, one of the most important measures to meet environmental objectives is to change forest policy instruments in order to obtain cost-effective instruments for a sustainable forest management. The Green Party wants a system that provides increased profitability for those who manage their forests with extra consideration for biological and social values. We're not there today. Mrs. Old President! The Green Party has a forestry policy with high ambitions for the protection of areas for the conservation of animal and plant species and valuable nature. As a minimum, we need to follow the strategic plan, the Nagoya Protocol, adopted in 2010 under the UN Convention on Biological Diversity. This means that Sweden will protect 17% of all land and freshwater areas in the long term by 2020. These areas shall be of biological value. Currently, only about 5% of the productive forest land is covered by statutory protection. The protection is also unevenly distributed – large areas of coniferous forests are protected near the mountain chain while about 2 per cent are protected in the rest of Sweden. The Green Party considered in 2014 and still believes that the milestones need to be increased and that additional funds need to be allocated to achieve the objectives of the Nagoya Protocol. We also want state forest land to be used as exchange land for those forest owners who want other land rather than money. But, Madam President, protecting forests is not enough to meet national and international environmental objectives. A transition to sustainable use of forest land is also necessary. Through the Nagoya Protocol, Sweden has undertaken to manage areas that are used for forestry in a sustainable manner by 2020 – it is not far to go – in order to ensure the conservation of biodiversity. It is therefore not only a question of leaving untouched areas, but also of managing the forest that is managed in other ways. Trakthyggesbruket has been applied on a large scale since the 1950s and has brought revolutionary changes to the Swedish forest landscape. Forests with different kinds of trees of different ages have been replaced by plantings of corpse trees. The Green Party wants to see farming methods that create forests with different-aged trees, leaves and left-over dead wood. Non-poor forestry practices should be promoted, as they have great benefits for biodiversity, outdoor life, tourism, reindeer nutrition and cultural values. Interest in clean forestry is growing. I'm glad to hear that. At the same time, however, it is still a very small part, only a few percent, of the productive forest land that is managed by some kind of decent forestry method. The Swedish Environmental Protection Agency's in-depth follow-up in 2015, as I mentioned earlier, states that a significant increase in areas that are managed using non-slip methods is needed. It also notes the need for new and reinforced measures to this end. The Environmental Party therefore believes that the Swedish Forest Agency's funding for advice on non-slip farming practices is important and should be increased. We also think that those forest owners who want to switch from rural to non-housing forestry should be able to receive conversion aid just as it is now possible to obtain aid for conversion from conventional to organic farming. We also believe that the state should set an example in its forestry. Sveaskog, Fortificationsverket and Statens Fastighetsverk should therefore give priority to clean methods. We believe that targets for clean wood should be set for state forest holdings. Mrs. Old President! Key biotopes, i.e. forest areas with very high natural values, play an important role in preserving the endangered plants and animals of the forest. These areas have been surveyed using a common method nationally since the 1990s. Studies show that there are many areas with key biotopes quality that have not yet been recorded. The pace of the key biotop inventory should therefore be increased in order to reduce the risk that valuable areas are accidentally harvested. The fact that Skogsstyrelsens gd instead goes out and pauses the inventory in large parts of Sweden is completely baroque and has aroused great criticism from environmental organisations, researchers and the Swedish Forest Agency's own officials. In the collaboration group set up to develop the methods for the key biotop inventory, both environmental organisations and researchers have left the discussions. The Board of Forests has tried to take its gd in the ear and decided on Tuesday at its board meeting that the inventory should be resumed, however without any registrations to be made. The strange move from Skogsstyrelsen's gd has diluted the already polarised forest debate. It's very troubling, I think. And the question is also what measures the government intends to take to ensure the survival of fish farms and to ensure that they can develop and expand their activities.

i-0a2e44a73839b312-1319	Mrs. Old President! During the previous debate, we talked about the importance of protecting forests and land. At least that's what you see with your naked eye. I would like to support our Amendment No 12, which states that a ban on bottom trawling should be introduced in Sweden and that we should also be promoting such a ban at European level. Fishing with bottom trawl means that you have a wide, large and heavy yarn that sweeps over the bottom, stirs it up and takes with you a lot of the living environments that are there. Just as Moderates Jesper Skalberg Karlsson said earlier, it is quite clear that this has an environmental impact. That is also why we in Sweden have extended the trawl line as far as possible only under EU legislation. In the area of Öresund where there has been a ban on bottom trawling since the 1930s you can see that there is a whole different life on the bottom. Fish stocks are much more alive there than in other comparable areas. Therefore, a ban on bottom trawling should be introduced. Sometimes it is said that today's trawls are much better than yesterday's, that they can be more selective and so on, and that is a good thing. Sometimes there may be a discussion about whether it is bottom trawls at all. But if the new tools are so refined and better than conventional yarns, I would not call them bottom trawls in the traditional sense. The report also addresses the EU's external fisheries policy and the so-called fisheries partner agreements that the EU has with some fourteen poor countries, preferably African countries. We in the Left Party believe that these agreements are a colonial remnant, that this applies primarily to countries that have previously been Spanish or Portuguese colonies with which agreements have been concluded. These are areas where the colonial powers have previously fished, and they want to be able to continue to do so when European fishing waters are largely depleted. We spend a lot of money on our tax resources to finance the neo-colonial fisheries agreements. We in the Left believe that the only thing to do is to take this money and instead invest it in developing locally sustainable fisheries in these countries: Senegal, Mauritania, São Tomé and Príncipe, Tanzania and so on. These countries have never been offered it, but are always offered new fisheries agreements, in which European fleets are to fish their fish. It is difficult for a poor country, which is perhaps also corrupt and top-down, to say no when you get millions of euros from Brussels. Our reservation in this area is about abolishing these agreements and turning them into development projects for local and sustainable fisheries. Mrs. Old President! I would also like to mention the report that we in the Committee on the Environment and Agriculture have produced and which is annexed. It is a review that we in the committee have carried out of the system of transferable fishing rights in pelagic fisheries. Pelagic fishing applies to fish that swim freely in the sea. It is mainly about herring, sprat and mackerel. The system of simply transferring and selling fishing rights to other fishing companies was introduced and clubbed in the Swedish Parliament in 2009. I have chaired the evaluation group which produced this report, which is annexed to the report. I am not going to draw the whole content but will briefly mention some of the conclusions we have reached. We note that the introduction and implementation of this system of transferable rights has worked well and has fulfilled its purpose. This has also meant that the share of pelagic fishing vessels has halved and that the concentration of ownership in pelagic fisheries has increased. Those who are active in herring and mackerel fishing are generally satisfied with the system, but as far as small-scale and coastal fisheries are concerned, we note that there is a relatively widespread criticism. We say that a simple regulatory framework is important. Where you can make rules changes. Of course, however, the purpose of laws and regulations must be maintained. We also say that it may be a good idea to try camera control on the fishing boats to ensure that the landing of the fish is as it was intended to be. Finally, we note that the time for the system of transferable fishing rights expires in 2019. Of course, many in the industry want to know what will happen in the future. We say that, in good time, a parliamentary working party should be set up to continue to review this system in order to be able to take a decision on the future of the system in good time.

The objective of the EU Common Fisheries Policy (CFP) is to ensure that fisheries and aquaculture are environmentally sustainable in the long term and managed in a manner consistent with the objectives of economic, social and employment benefits and of contributing to food security. The Common Fisheries Policy (CFP) states that the EU will seek to ensure that fisheries agreements with non-EU countries are of benefit to both the EU and the third countries concerned. The EU shall actively support and contribute to the development of scientific knowledge and advice. According to the Maritime and Water Authority's report on the Common Fisheries Policy, it has been clear that the new requirements of the basic Regulation have been consistently complied with in the context of the negotiating mandates and protocols for sustainable fisheries partnership agreements and protocols discussed in the Council in 2015. The Council Regulation establishing a common system to prevent, justify and eliminate illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing means, inter alia, that if a third country does not have fisheries management in line with international rules and basic principles, it is possible to prohibit imports from that country. It is important that Sweden continues to be a driving force for the decisions taken within the EU to lead to sustainable management of the fish stock both within the EU and globally. Mrs. Old President! One of the elements for achieving the objectives of sustainable fisheries is fisheries control. In Sweden, the Agency for Marine and Water Management has the overall responsibility for the mainly EU-regulated fisheries control. Fisheries control is managed through annual monitoring plans covering all fisheries control activities. The Swedish Agency for Marine and Water Management is changing its way of working towards a more risk-based approach, which means focusing control on those areas where the risk of non-compliance is most assessed. Since 1 May 2007, there has been a general ban on fishing eel for those without a special permit. In order to be authorised to fish eel, one must have had such a permit in the past. Some fresh water in Sweden is exempted from the ban. It is therefore possible for recreational fishermen to fish for eel. As a result of the stricter rules, the number of eel fishers has halved since 2006. Catches have also been halved during the same period. I want to finish where I started. It is gratifying that pelagic fishing works mainly, mainly thanks to transferable fishing rights. I believe that such rights also come in demersal fisheries. It's necessary in the long term. But it must come from below, as it did with pelagic fishing, not from above politicians and authorities. There is a positive development between the Maritime and Water Authority and fishermen. This dialogue, which we have missed in the past, is very positive.
